A's readiness command is three constants today (deploy.go:698-703), so nothing moves for A.
- **model: Writer Word**
  - Was: func Word(s string, allowed ...string): a bare token from a closed list M checks, offered to a tool for the enums of a Custom body, so nothing a tool supplies reaches the text both unquoted and unchecked.
  - Should be: The caller supplies the list, so Word(v, v) writes any text bare. Word must also hold s to a fixed token grammar of its own (a letter, then letters and digits) and refuse every word a YAML 1.1 reader takes for a non-string: true, false, yes, no, on, off, y, n, null, in any case. Or keep bare enums inside M's typed documents and give a Custom body Str for everything.
  - Evidence: The model's signature. The YAML 1.1 list A already guards against is plainNotString (A internal/consolidate/consolidate.go:597). B's bare enums in the body are pullPolicy and updateStrategy (B internal/render/render.go:95,143); quoted, the API receives the same strings.
- **model: Manifest.Render errors; difference 'Render errors'**
  - Was: Every grammar failure is an error naming the kind, the object, the field, the value and the rule, never a secret value (the never-a-value rule is stated for Secret.Data only).
  - Should be: The value is echoed only for names, keys and enum words. For a payload field the error names the field, the key and the rule and never the value: Secret data, RegistrySecret user and password, ConfigMap data, Env values, Command and Args, annotation values and Custom body scalars.
  - Evidence: A's ConfigMap value application.yml carries the status account password as a literal: 'on kubernetes the literal sits in a ConfigMap' (A internal/gen/gen.go:257-258; deploy.go:502-509). A's JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS and JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS values are operator text (deploy.go:626-633). A's existing rule: 'Errors name the stable secret name and the variable, never a value' (gen.go:794-796).
- **model: Container.Extra (generic slot); owner questions 12 and 13**
  - Was: Container keys M does not model; a key M writes is refused, securityContext included.
  - Should be: Also refuse envFrom, or the slot undoes the model's own rule 'no valueFrom and no envFrom: secrets arrive as files'. Better an allow list of harmless keys (workingDir, lifecycle, terminationMessagePath, stdin, tty) than a deny list: it fails closed when Kubernetes adds a field.
  - Evidence: A's rule for its Deployment: credentials are files, 'never injected as environment variables, where any child process or crash dump would see them' (deploy.go:645-647). B records why envFrom is dangerous: it turns every key of the named Secret into an environment variable (B internal/k8s/secrets.go:89-92). env is M-written and so refused; envFrom is not a key M writes, so the slot admits it.
- **model: Map/Seq rule 'a key whose Map or Seq is empty is dropped'; difference 'A key with nothing under it'**
  - Was: The writer drops a key whose mapping or sequence is empty; '{}' appears only through EmptyMap.
  - Should be: Drop silently only inside M's typed documents. In a tool-built tree (Custom.Body, Affinity.Extra, Pod.Extra, Container.Extra, PersistentVolume.Source) an empty Map or Seq is an error naming the key, and the tool chooses between leaving the key out and EmptyMap.
  - Evidence: B: the CRD requires spec on every nodeAssignment item (operator-1.4.2.yaml.tmpl:1271-1273) and B writes that header even with nothing under it (B internal/render/render.go:311-312); dropped, a role with no placement renders '- name: Backup' alone and the apply is refused. A: in a verbatim affinity block 'labelSelector: {}' selects every pod and an absent selector none, so a silent drop flips the meaning. A's renderer already writes such a value as a bare 'key:', a null (A internal/render/render.go:398-402): a latent A defect the model should fix, not inherit.
- **model: Writer Join together with Block's '|+'**
  - Was: Join trims trailing newlines of every document; Block writes '|+' when the content ends in more than one newline.
  - Should be: Render does not trim documents it wrote itself, or Block never ends in keep-chomping: content with more than one trailing newline falls back to Str. Trimming is right only for foreign text (Raw).
  - Evidence: ConfigMap data is sorted, so the last key (A: status, deploy.go:520-527) is the last node of its document. A '|+' scalar there owns the trailing blank lines, and Join's trim removes them, so the cluster holds other bytes than the tool passed. A cannot hit this today because it always clips to one newline (A internal/yamlwriter/yamlwriter.go:35-41; deploy.go:502-527).
- **difference 'Writing generate output to a file'**
  - Was: Both call M's one atomic write (gdu 7) with mode 0600: temp file, chmod, rename. The overwrite question stays in each tool.
  - Should be: M's write refuses a target that is a symbolic link (Lstat before the temp file), creates the temp file 0600 before its first byte, then renames. Only the overwrite question stays in the tool. Written that way A gains the refusal and B cannot lose it in adoption.
  - Evidence: B refuses a symlink target because 'the artifact carries secrets, and writing through a link an operator did not notice puts them wherever it points' (B internal/cli/helpers.go:147-152), then CreateTemp, Chmod 0600, Rename (helpers.go:177-194). The sequence gdu 7 lifts lists temp file, mode, write, sync, rename and no symlink rule (M docs/todo.md:421-422). With rename alone a link at the output path is silently replaced by a regular file in the link's own directory; A follows the link today (os.WriteFile, A cmd/solmq-conn-util/main.go:1776), so its secret-bearing manifest would move to a place the operator did not choose.
- **extras: A's verbatim kubernetes.deployment.affinity block through Affinity.Extra**
  - Was: A converts the yaml.v3 node to M's value tree and passes it in Affinity.Extra; M writes it.
  - Should be: State the conversion rule: by the node's resolved tag, an integer to Int, a boolean to Bool, an empty mapping to EmptyMap, everything else to Str. With Str for every scalar, 'weight: 50' becomes the string "50" and the API refuses the Deployment.
  - Evidence: A writes plain scalars of the block as they stand (FormatScalar default branch, A internal/consolidate/consolidate.go:585-586), so an integer weight stays an integer today; the typed spread term beside it writes 'weight: 100' bare (deploy.go:224). Validate checks the block's keys and outline only: 'What a term says is the API server's to check' (A internal/validate/validate.go:1378-1381, 1415-1432).
- **model: Custom.Body for B's custom resource; owner question 1**
  - Was: Every string a tool supplies goes through Str; the Body note lists the bare values that become quoted.
  - Should be: Name the bare values that must stay numbers. B holds them as config strings and must parse them to Int: securityContext.runAsUser and fsGroup, brokerContainerSecurity.runAsUser and runAsGroup, service.ports[].containerPort and servicePort. Through Str they reach integer fields of the CRD as strings and the apply is refused.
  - Evidence: B internal/render/render.go:219,222 (port numbers are strings out of parsePort, render.go:1249-1263) and render.go:268-282 (ids written with %s from string fields).
- **model: Writer Block, indentation indicator**
  - Was: An indentation indicator is written when the first line starts with a space.
  - Should be: When the first NON-EMPTY line starts with a space, or a line before it holds only spaces. A reader takes the block's indentation from the first non-empty line, and a leading blank line longer than that is an error.
  - Evidence: YAML block scalar rule (knowledge, not a file). A writes no indicator today (deploy.go:502-527), safe only because application.yml, the logback XML and the status script start at column 0.

Missed:

- (A) Verbatim affinity block: a sequence item that is a mapping is written as a lone '-' line with the mapping two columns deeper, not with its first key on the dash line. The census says only 'sequences as - item'. A tree writer changes these bytes. [A internal/render/render.go:405-413]
- (A) Verbatim affinity block: a value that is an empty mapping or an empty sequence ('labelSelector: {}', 'namespaces: []') is written as a bare 'key:' with nothing under it, a null. The operator's '{}' is lost today. [A internal/render/render.go:398-402]
- (A) Verbatim affinity block: a scalar holding a newline is re-emitted as a block scalar, '|' or '|-' by its trailing newline, body two columns deeper, for mapping values and sequence items alike. A second block-scalar writer beside the ConfigMap one. [A internal/render/render.go:36-53, 419-427]
- (A) Guards the census left unopened. Affinity keys are held to ^[A-Za-z0-9]([-A-Za-z0-9_./]*[A-Za-z0-9])?$ at every depth, anchors and aliases are refused, only nodeAffinity, podAffinity and podAntiAffinity may sit at the top; affinity VALUES are not checked. Toleration operator is held to unset, Equal or Exists and effect to unset or the three effects, so the two fields listed as 'written with no transform' are closed enums; Equal with an empty value is accepted, and a node-selector value may be empty. [A internal/validate/validate.go:1320-1368, 1376-1432]
- (A) A write to the cluster outside the manifest: the status script is installed into the running connector container through kube.Upload (sh -c with mkdir -p and cat, body on stdin). Not a rendered artifact, but a file written into the pod with whatever mode the container's umask gives. [A internal/runner/runner.go:1157-1181; M kube/kube.go:211-226]
- (B) Removal of Secrets does not go through a manifest: broker remove runs 'kubectl delete secret <name> -n <ns> --ignore-not-found' once per owned Secret, built with c.kubectl and not an M builder. The same holds for data PVCs (delete pvc), a pod restart (delete pod) and file transfer (kubectl cp, both ways). The census notes say one non-manifest write exists (set env). [B internal/k8s/prep.go:273-287; internal/k8s/deploy.go:215; internal/k8s/ops.go:138; internal/k8s/transport.go:106,116]
- (B) operator generate without registry credentials returns the template output untouched, and the embedded bundle has no final newline (its last line, 1967, 'terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 10', has no line end; wc counts 1966). That stream ends without a newline; with credentials joinYAMLDocs ends it with one. [B internal/k8s/operator.go:146-148, 411-424; internal/k8s/assets/operator-1.4.2.yaml.tmpl:1967]
- (B) The 'spec:' line under each nodeAssignment item is a key the CRD requires (required: name, spec), not a stray header. The census records it only as 'written even when nothing follows'. [B internal/render/render.go:311-312; internal/k8s/assets/operator-1.4.2.yaml.tmpl:1271-1273]

Notes: Read-only: nothing was run, built or changed. Opened: design note (whole), decisions.md (whole), todo.md items 1, 4, 7, 18; A deploy.go and yamlwriter.go (whole), A render.go 36-53 and 370-427, consolidate.go 560-640, validate.go 317-337, 893-975, 1015-1110, 1300-1440, 1500-1560, gen.go 225-332, 460-525, 775-815, 1100-1220, spec/runas.go, spec/image.go, main.go 1760-1785, namespace.go 50-72, runner.go 1150-1185; B render.go 1-520, 680-700, 1240-1304, secrets.go and operator.go (whole), prep.go 120-430, deploy.go 14-50 and 176-222, cli/helpers.go 125-205, config/validate.go 640-684, the bundle's kind lines and lines 1-8, 298-330, 1265-1275, 1505-1512, 1724-1736, 1896-1967; M kube/kube.go (whole), compose.go 20-80, artifacttest.go 1-60. 1. Censuses. Field by field, A's census matches deploy.go and B's matches BrokerCR, secrets.go and M's four-line Namespace document; the bundle applies exactly the eight kinds listed. What was missed is layout and sinks, listed under missed. Small accuracy points: the bundle has seven {{.Namespace}} lines, as B's census says (the code comment at operator.go:30 says six). 2. Representability. Apart from the corrections (run-as 0:0, name-only documents, the affinity conversion, B's numeric strings, B's required spec key), every census field maps to a model field, an extra, or a listed difference. Checked specifically: A's projected stores volume with one source still renders projected because every source carries items (deploy.go:60-89, 730-737); ConfigMap keys are already in bytewise order; pod labels likewise; '- secret:' at 14 with children at 18 is what a tree writer produces. 3. Posture. These proposals hold or improve what the tools do: one quoting rule through strconv.Quote (M compose.go:30-44 already documents the invalid UTF-8 exception), data-only Secrets from bytes, RegistrySecret refusing empty input, Refs taking application.yml and its status password out of A's delete stream, read-only mounts by default, 0400 default, automount false, seccomp RuntimeDefault, the three container securityContext lines identical to deploy.go:267-274, absolute mount paths, no hostPath field. '$' doubling for env, command and args is right: the kubelet reads '$$' as '$' everywhere. Already decided and not reopened: the labelled Namespace in generate output, with its hand-apply effect recorded (todo.md:119-122). 4. Smaller points, not weakenings of today: - defaultMode 0400: a YAML 1.2 reader takes it for decimal 400, mode 0620; decimal 256 cannot be misread. A writes 0400 today (deploy.go:719,728), so owner question 16 is a real choice. Mode's ceiling of 0777 lets a tool make secret files world-readable: consider refusing 'other' bits on a Secret volume. - Custom's refusal of typed kinds does not deliver 'cannot be written around M's hardening': Raw accepts any kind (B's bundle needs a Raw Deployment, tmpl:1897-1967) and Custom accepts Pod, StatefulSet, DaemonSet or Job with a hand-written template. The guarantee has to come from the artifacttest check, which must then require the negatives, not only forbid tokens. - That check must match key-and-value tokens, not bare words: the bundle's CRD text names HostNetwork in a description (tmpl:1363). - Objects cannot describe a Raw document (kind only), so B's operatorProbes list (operator.go:312-329) stays hand-written. - Map needs a duplicate-key refusal: B writes configured load-balancer annotations after the metallb keys in one mapping (render.go:484-494), and an entry can repeat one. - M cannot enforce 'ownership labels never in podLabels' inside Custom.Body. Export the reserved-key check so B applies it to kubernetes.podLabels (render.go:205-206). - PersistentVolume.Source names 'local' as an example: it is a host directory like hostPath, and needs a nodeAffinity the model has no field for. - Entry.Key is a plain string: the model does not say how the writer tells M's own bare keys from tool keys that must be quoted (a Custom body's 'spec', B's free-text annotation keys).

## 4. Env keys (gdu 19)

A has 110 platform keys, B has 129.

### 4.1 Alignment

- **[all] Key spelling (case convention)**: align-to-A
  - A: kebab-case for every key the tool defines: project-name, run-as, node-selector, image-pull, user-env (30 multi-word yaml tags in A:internal/spec). Kubernetes' own field names appear only inside an entry copied in the API's shape (tolerations[].tolerationSeconds, the affinity kinds). One outlier outside the platform sections: workflows.file_pattern (n/a; A camelCase spelling of a tool key is an error (A:docs/troubleshooting.md:269-270))
  - B: camelCase for every multi-word key: adminPass, passEnv, runUser, dataDir, quadletDir, imagePullSecret, healthCheck.startPeriod (71 camelCase yaml tags in B:internal/config, none kebab-case) (n/a; Strict decode: any other spelling is an unknown key)
  - Difference: Every multi-word key is spelled differently, so none can be the same key in both files until one convention wins. The one key already decided for both tools, docker.project-name, is kebab-case.
  - Reason: The decided project key is already kebab-case, and A's rule (tool keys kebab-case, Kubernetes' own field names only inside a block passed on in the API's shape) also fits B's custom-resource pass-through blocks.
- **[all] Secret given as a literal or as an environment reference (the key pair)**: align-to-A
  - A: <key> and <key>-env (image.pass / image.pass-env, tls.stores.<name>.password / password-env, every connection credential) (none; One form only, through envref.Ref.Check; -env is a variable name, not ${...}, not starting _GEN_; an unset variable warns at lint and fails only at deploy (A:internal/validate/validate.go:404-431))
  - B: <key> and <key>Env (image.pass / image.passEnv, semp.adminPass / adminPassEnv, redundancy.psk / pskEnv, tls.certPassphrase / certPassphraseEnv, additionalUsers[].password / passwordEnv) (none; Both set is an error; the variable is resolved in Load, so an unset or empty variable fails every command (B:internal/config/secretref.go:56-80))
  - Difference: Suffix spelling. When an unset variable is fatal: A at deploy only, B at every load, including remove.
  - Reason: The suffix follows the spelling rule, and resolving only when a value is needed is what gdu 3 already decided (remove resolves no credential, M:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:125-127).
- **[all] Unknown or misspelled key**: align-to-B
  - A: - (dropped silently today; env.yaml is decoded non-strict (A:internal/spec/env.go:52-56); only tls and java-options refuse unknown sub-keys. A's own review already decided CR-0046: an unknown key is a validate error with a near-match hint (A:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:62))
  - B: - (hard error; Strict decode: an unknown or misspelled key fails the load (B:env/sample.yaml:12-14))
  - Difference: A accepts a file B's rule would refuse. Until CR-0046 lands in A, a key renamed by this table vanishes there without a message.
  - Reason: A has already decided it (CR-0046), and with strict decode in both tools every rename in this table fails loudly by itself.
- **[all] Removed and renamed keys that are still parsed in order to be refused**: already-aligned
  - A: docker and podman: image, timezone, secrets, stores, libs.mount-path; podman.mode, podman.quadlet; kubernetes.deployment.image, deployment.timezone, kubernetes.logging, kubernetes.secrets.stores, secrets.credentials.create.{source,variables,values-file}; ignored with a lint note: podman.base-dir, credentials.create.name, libs.pvc.create.name, image-pull.name with create (never defaulted; A kept field per removed key, refused by name in validate (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1718-1760, 1840-1860))
  - B: admin, kubernetes.runtime, docker.runtime, podman.runtime, podman.rootless, docker.composeFile, <docker|podman>.container.shmSize, container.ulimits.nofile, container.ulimits.memlock, kubernetes.msgNode.cpu, kubernetes.containerSecurity.readOnlyRootFilesystem, scaling.maxPool, scaling.cpu and scaling.messagingNodeCpu (denied) (never defaulted; A kept field per removed key, refused by name with its replacement (B:internal/config/config.go:695-702, 976-986, 1032-1043))
  - Difference: Same mechanism in both tools, different lists. gdu 19 adds a rename for most platform keys, so the lists would roughly double if every rename got its own detector.
  - Reason: The mechanism is the same today; whether the detectors survive the breaking tag is an owner question below, since migration is out of scope and strict decode already reports the key.
- **[all] Expansion inside a value (${VAR} and a leading ~)**: align-to-B
  - A: - (${VAR} and ${VAR:default} expand in every non-credential string field; no ~ expansion; Fields tagged expand:"no" and verbatim YAML are left alone)
  - B: - (a leading ~ expands in path keys and in command arguments after the first; $ is literal text; -)
  - Difference: A expands variables, B does not.
  - Reason: Decided under gdu 2 (M:docs/todo.md:161-177): A stops expanding ${VAR}, both expand only a leading ~ through the new tilde package, and each tool keeps its own list of path keys.
- **[all] Which platform section a run uses**: already-aligned
  - A: top-level kubernetes, docker, podman (every section optional; defaults applied only to a section that is present (A:internal/spec/env.go:69-80); --platform, else resolved from the sections present (A:docs/commands.md:19-22))
  - B: top-level kubernetes, docker, podman (a section must be written even when empty (docker: {}); --platform, else the one section present; several ask, and fail without a terminal (B:docs/configuration.md:150-166))
  - Difference: None in the keys.
  - Reason: Both tools name the three sections the same way and pick the platform from the flag or from the sections present.
- **[all] Relative and absolute host paths**: inherent
  - A: store files, <docker|podman>.libs.dir, workflows.dir (relative paths resolve against the env file's directory (A:docs/configuration.md:50-51); safeHostPath on libs.dir and on docker store paths)
  - B: tls.cert, tls.certKey, tls.cas; <docker|podman>.container.dataDir; podman.quadletDir, podman.baseDir (tls paths are rebased onto the env file's directory; dataDir, quadletDir and baseDir are never rebased (B:docs/configuration.md:86-87); CheckHostPath; dataDir and baseDir must be absolute)
  - Difference: A accepts a relative bind-mount source (libs.dir); B refuses a relative dataDir.
  - Reason: The broker keeps its state in dataDir and remove --delete-data empties it, so B refuses a path that moves with the env file, while A's libs directory is read-only input.
- **[all] Ownership labels and version annotations**: already-aligned
  - A: - (fixed labels today: solace-connector/le-mode, /role and the two digest labels; no key)
  - B: - (only the deploy-digest label today; no key)
  - Difference: None in the keys: the label vocabulary is fixed in M and the instance value is the name key.
  - Reason: Decided under gdu 1: M writes the labels and annotations when it renders, and neither env file gains a key for them.
- **[shared] Image block is required**: already-aligned
  - A: image (none; Required whenever a platform is in play; skipped for generate config (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1521-1535))
  - B: image (none; image.repo and image.tag mandatory on kubernetes, docker and podman)
  - Difference: None in the block; the sub-keys differ (next rows).
  - Reason: One top-level image block serves every platform in both tools.
- **[shared] Registry host**: align-to-B
  - A: image.repo (empty = Docker Hub; the pull-Secret auths key is then https://index.docker.io/v1/ (A:internal/spec/image.go:9, 83-88); SafeToken; host and port only, a Hub namespace belongs in image.name; trailing / trimmed (A:internal/spec/image.go:30, 59-76))
  - B: image.registry (none (no prefix); imageRegistryRE: host, optional :port, optional path (B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:32-34). Used as the login server (B:internal/container/manager.go:464-468), written as-is as the auths key of the pull Secret (B:internal/k8s/secrets.go:254-256), and prefixed onto kubernetes.operator.image)
  - Difference: Different key for the same thing, and A's word repo is B's word for the repository. B also allows a path prefix and writes an empty auths key when the registry is unset.
  - Reason: registry names exactly this in both tools, while repo cannot stay in either file because it means the registry in A and the repository in B; the value rule should be A's (host and port only, Docker Hub's key when empty) since M will write the one registry payload.
- **[shared] Image repository path**: new-common-key
  - A: image.name (none; Required; SafeToken (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1536-1555))
  - B: image.repo (none; Required; imageRepoRE: lowercase path, optionally led by a registry host when image.registry is unset (B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:36-38))
  - Difference: Different key, and image.repo exists in both files with two meanings. B lets the registry ride inside the repository.
  - Reason: image.repository is the OCI word and the custom resource's own field, it collides with no existing key, so an old file fails as an unknown key in both tools instead of being read with the other meaning.
- **[shared] Image tag**: inherent
  - A: image.tag (none; Required (untagged refused); SafeToken; a value starting sha256: is joined with @ (A:internal/spec/image.go:69-76))
  - B: image.tag (none; Required; imageTagRE, a digest is not expressible (B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:40-42); the built-in health check needs a version tag of 10.26 or later)
  - Difference: Same key. A accepts a digest pin, B does not.
  - Reason: The operator's custom resource takes repository and tag as two fields (B:internal/render/render.go:87-88) and B's version-gated features read the broker version from the tag, so a digest has no place in B; the key is already the same.
- **[shared] Registry account name**: align-to-A
  - A: image.user, image.user-env (none; Literal or reference, never both; required only with kubernetes.secrets.image-pull.create (A:internal/spec/image.go:34-35; A:internal/validate/validate.go:1559, 1576-1578))
  - B: image.user (none; No rule found; no reference form (B:internal/config/config.go:305))
  - Difference: B has no reference form for the account name.
  - Reason: One pair rule for every credential position lets an env file keep the registry account out of the file in both tools, at the cost of one entry in B's reference list.
- **[shared] Registry password**: align-to-A
  - A: image.pass, image.pass-env (none; As image.user (A:internal/spec/image.go:36-37))
  - B: image.pass, image.passEnv (none; No control character; both set is an error; resolved at load (B:internal/config/config.go:306-307; B:internal/config/secretref.go:25))
  - Difference: Suffix spelling only.
  - Reason: Follows the pair spelling of the second row.
- **[shared] What the registry credential does on docker and podman**: align-to-B
  - A: image.user, image.pass (nothing: the host must already be logged in (A:docs/configuration.md:1042); A's example file calls the login an open decision (A:internal/examples/files/env.yaml:246-253); Read only to build the kubernetes pull Secret)
  - B: image.user, image.pass (<engine> login --username U --password-stdin [registry] before deploy (B:internal/container/manager.go:456-475); Both or neither)
  - Difference: The same two keys have an effect on one platform in A and on three in B. The parity body lists this under Inherent differences (M:paritydoc/body.md:200-201), but its reason (A pulls, B logs in) does not stop A from logging in.
  - Reason: A pulls the image to read its entrypoint and so needs the host logged in anyway, and the same keys then mean the same thing on every platform in both tools.
- **[shared] Container timezone**: already-aligned
  - A: timezone (empty = no TZ set; SafeToken when set (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1528-1530))
  - B: timezone (none; no TZ line and no CR field; checkTimezone: UTF-8, no control, invisible, whitespace or execguard.UnsafeChars character (B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:54-80))
  - Difference: Same key and default; two near-identical charset checks.
  - Reason: The key, the place and the default match, and the one charset check can move into M with rendering.
- **[shared] JVM options, actuator port and syslog shipping**: inherent
  - A: java-options.tool, java-options.jdk, management.port, logging.syslog.{host,port,protocol} (empty; 8090; syslog off, protocol udp; Charset rule on the options; syslog host pattern and port 1-65535; management.port unchecked)
  - B: - (-; -)
  - Difference: A only.
  - Reason: JVM options, the Spring actuator port and logback syslog exist only for the Java connector.
- **[shared] TLS material**: inherent
  - A: tls.stores.<name>.{password, password-env, existing.pkcs12, existing.secret.{name,key}, create.{certs,keys,jks}}; selected per connection by truststore, keystore, key-alias, mq.tls and a tcps:// host (none; Store name is a DNS-like label up to 63; create or existing; password pair rule)
  - B: tls.cert, tls.certKey, tls.cas, tls.certPassphrase, tls.certPassphraseEnv (none; cert and certKey together or neither; CheckHostPath; passphrase pair rule)
  - Difference: Both use a top-level tls block, with different content: named PKCS12 client stores selected per connection, against one server certificate as PEM files.
  - Reason: The connector is a TLS client that needs named trust and key stores per connection, while the broker is a TLS server with one certificate, so only the spelling rule applies (cert-key, cert-passphrase-env).
- **[shared] Application credentials**: inherent
  - A: connections.<name>.solace.client-username, client-password; connections.<name>.mq.user, password; leader-election.session.*; security.users[].password; each with its -env form (none; Pair rule; delivered as mounted secret files on every platform)
  - B: semp.adminPass, semp.monitorPass, semp.additionalUsers[].{username,accessLevel,password}, redundancy.psk; each secret with its Env form (none; adminPass mandatory on docker and podman; pair rule; no control character)
  - Difference: Different accounts for different applications.
  - Reason: Each tool delivers the accounts of its own application, and the part they share is the pair rule of the second row.
- **[shared] High availability mode**: inherent
  - A: leader-election.mode (standalone, active_active, active_standby) with kubernetes.deployment.replicas (standalone; replicas 1; standalone with replicas above 1 is an error)
  - B: redundancy.enabled, redundancy.<primary|backup|monitor>.{name,addr} ("false"; Node names and addresses mandatory on docker and podman in HA)
  - Difference: Different models: replicas of one Deployment electing a leader, against a fixed group of three broker nodes.
  - Reason: Connector leader election and the broker's primary, backup and monitor group are each product's own HA design.
- **[shared] Broker scaling tier and limits**: inherent
  - A: - (-; -)
  - B: scaling.maxConnections, maxSpoolUsageMB, maxQueueMessages, maxKafkaBridge, maxKafkaConnections, maxBridges, maxSubscriptions, maxGuaranteedMsgMB, each also under its broker setting name (tier 100 on kubernetes, 1000 on docker and podman; maxConnections one of five tiers; closed key set; two spellings of one setting refused)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: The scaling tier is a broker concept that fixes its cores and default memory, and the connector has nothing like it.
- **[shared] Access path to a replication mate**: inherent
  - A: - (-; -)
  - B: replication.sites[].via.kubernetes.{command,namespace,name}; replication.sites[].via.semp.passSecret.{namespace,name,key} (none; command through execguard with the kubernetes allowlist; passSecret parts held to the Kubernetes name grammars)
  - Difference: B only. The block already repeats the kubernetes section's own words command, namespace and name.
  - Reason: Disaster-recovery replication between two brokers is a broker feature, and its block keeps following the kubernetes section's key names after this alignment.
- **[docker+podman] Shape of the docker and podman sections**: align-to-A
  - A: flat: <platform>.command, name, restart, run-as, ports, libs.dir (A:internal/spec/targets.go:201-217, 239-268) (n/a; -)
  - B: nested: <platform>.command, <platform>.network.{mode,ports}, <platform>.container.{name,runUser,mem,cpuset,monitorCpuset,dataDir,ulimits.core,healthCheck.*} (B:internal/config/config.go:965-972, 1046-1056) (n/a; -)
  - Difference: B puts the instance's settings one level down in container and the ports under network; A keeps them directly in the section.
  - Reason: A section deploys exactly one container, so the container level names nothing, and flat keys line up with kubernetes.name and kubernetes.namespace.
- **[docker+podman+kubernetes] Engine command**: align-to-B
  - A: docker.command, podman.command, kubernetes.command (docker, podman, kubectl; A string split on whitespace; execguard with allowlists {docker}, {podman}, {kubectl, oc}; --allow-command widens (A:internal/validate/validate.go:47-57))
  - B: docker.command, podman.command, kubernetes.command (docker, podman, kubectl; A scalar split on whitespace, or a list of exact arguments (B:internal/config/config.go:234-261); allowlists {docker, docker-compose, nerdctl}, {podman}, {kubectl, oc} (B:internal/config/execguard.go:38-42); leading ~ expanded in arguments)
  - Difference: Same key on all three platforms. B also accepts a list; the docker allowlist differs.
  - Reason: The list form is the only way to write an argument that holds a space, and one allowlist per platform should hold in both tools once both run the same M commands.
- **[docker] Compose invocation**: align-to-B
  - A: - (the tool appends the word compose to docker.command (A:internal/runner/runner.go:179); -)
  - B: docker.compose (docker.command plus compose; execguard with the docker allowlist; the one bare word allowed is compose as the last token (B:internal/config/execguard.go:64-71))
  - Difference: A has no key and cannot use a standalone compose binary.
  - Reason: M's containercli.Client takes the engine and the compose tokens separately (design 3.4), so the key costs A one default and lets a host with only the standalone binary deploy.
- **[docker] Compose project name (decided)**: align-to-A
  - A: docker.project-name (solace-ibmmq-connectors (A:internal/spec/targets.go:44, 284-286); DNS-1123 label (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1814-1816); written as the file's top-level name: (A:internal/dockergen/dockergen.go:101-103); no -p yet, and COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME overrides it (A:docs/configuration.md:857-858))
  - B: - today; decided: docker.project-name (today derived from docker.container.name, lowercased and folded (B:internal/render/render.go:1030-1041, 1063); decided default solace-event-brokers; today none of its own; the override through COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME is kept on purpose (B:internal/render/render.go:1056-1062))
  - Difference: B has no key. Neither tool passes -p, and M's ComposeUp and ComposeDown take no project (M:containercli/containercli.go:97-113).
  - Reason: Decided on 2026-11-14 and 2026-11-15 (gdu 9, gdu 19): both tools read docker.project-name and pass it with -p, which also ends the COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME override, and B takes A's DNS-1123 label rule.
- **[docker+podman] Network (decided: one key in both tools)**: new-common-key
  - A: - ; proposed docker.network and podman.network (no network line in either artifact today; -)
  - B: <docker|podman>.network.mode; proposed docker.network and podman.network (bridge, filled into the config for both sections (B:internal/config/load.go:260-261); bridge or host only (B:internal/config/validate.go:1704-1708); host writes network_mode: host or Network=host, bridge writes no line (B:internal/render/render.go:978-984, 1141-1148))
  - Difference: A has no key. B's key has two words, and its bridge means "write no line", which under the 2026-11-15 decision is the absent key, not the word bridge.
  - Reason: A scalar <platform>.network directly in the section uses the engines' own name for it (--network, Network=), leaves no room for the joined and named forms the decision refuses, and makes B's old mapping fail to decode instead of changing meaning.
- **[docker+podman] Published ports**: align-to-A
  - A: <platform>.ports (none: nothing published (A:internal/spec/targets.go:270-276); List of an integer N (meaning N:N) or "host:container"; each side 1-65535 (A:internal/spec/targets.go:148-181; A:internal/validate/validate.go:1627-1641))
  - B: <platform>.network.ports (none: nothing published; List of "[ip:]host:container[/tcp|/udp]", either side a port or a lo-hi range, no bare port (B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:124-181); checked in either mode, written only in bridge mode)
  - Difference: Place (under network in B). Grammar: B has the bind address, the protocol and ranges; A has a bare integer, which the engines themselves read as a container port on a random host port.
  - Reason: The list sits beside the scalar network key as <platform>.ports, and M should hold one port grammar for both renderers, which is B's host:container form because A's bare integer contradicts the engines' own meaning of a bare port.
- **[docker+podman] Instance name on docker and podman**: align-to-A
  - A: docker.name, podman.name (connector (A:internal/spec/targets.go:281-283, 307-309); DNS-1123 label (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1732-1734))
  - B: docker.container.name, podman.container.name (solace (B:internal/config/load.go:247-248); ^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]*$, at most 100 characters (B:internal/config/validate.go:404-410))
  - Difference: Place and grammar: B allows capitals, dots, underscores and 100 characters.
  - Reason: The name becomes the deploy.solace.com/instance label value, the unit stem, the compose service key and the secret prefix, and a DNS-1123 label is valid in every one of those sinks, while the default stays each product's own word.
- **[docker+podman] Restart policy**: align-to-A
  - A: docker.restart, podman.restart (unless-stopped on docker, always on podman (A:internal/spec/targets.go:34-35); docker: compose's words no, always, unless-stopped, on-failure[:N]; podman: systemd's Restart= words (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1672-1700))
  - B: - (fixed restart: always (B:internal/render/render.go:1076) and Restart=always (B:internal/render/render.go:1010); -)
  - Difference: B has no key; its docker default also differs from A's.
  - Reason: M's compose and quadlet models take the restart word from the tool anyway (design 8.2) and nothing in the broker forces the policy to be fixed, so B gains both keys with always as its default.
- **[docker+podman] Identity the container runs as on docker and podman**: align-to-A
  - A: <platform>.run-as (1000:0 (A:internal/spec/runas.go:16); "uid", "uid:gid" or the word image; digits only, each id at most 2147483647; a bare uid means gid 0; uid 0 warns (A:internal/spec/runas.go:18-50))
  - B: <platform>.container.runUser (1000001:0 on docker and rootful podman, 1000:0 rootless (B:internal/config/load.go:312-324); ^[A-Za-z0-9._-]+(:[A-Za-z0-9._-]+)?$, so names pass (B:internal/config/validate.go:415); a bare uid writes User= and no Group= (B:internal/render/render.go:891, 903-906))
  - Difference: Key name, grammar (B accepts names, A accepts the word image), and what a bare uid means for the group.
  - Reason: It is the same setting on the same platforms, and only the default is forced by the images: the broker image's own uid is 1000001, and the connector image declares no user.
- **[podman] Rootful or rootless podman**: already-aligned
  - A: podman.rootful (false; rootless.Decide with the key name passed in; refused when it disagrees with the effective uid)
  - B: podman.rootful (false; The same through rootless.Decide; also picks the default runUser)
  - Difference: None.
  - Reason: Same key, same default and the same M check in both tools.
- **[podman] Quadlet unit directory**: align-to-A
  - A: - (podman.QuadletDir: /etc/containers/systemd for root, else $XDG_CONFIG_HOME or ~/.config, then containers/systemd; A removed podman.quadlet.dir on purpose (A:internal/spec/targets.go:219-235))
  - B: podman.quadletDir (the same default, from podman.QuadletDir and podman.SystemQuadletDir (B:internal/config/load.go:282; M:podman/podman.go:23, 140); CheckHostPath; never rebased)
  - Difference: B keeps an override A removed. The parity body records it as aligned with the override (M:paritydoc/body.md:148).
  - Reason: systemd's generator reads units only from its own directories, which podman.QuadletDir already returns for the invoking account in both tools, so the override mostly lets a file name a directory the unit is never loaded from.
- **[podman] Legacy host directory**: align-to-A
  - A: podman.base-dir (ignored; Warns under lint only (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1848-1851))
  - B: podman.baseDir (none; Absolute when set; deploy and remove delete a legacy key file under it (B:internal/config/config.go:1005-1024))
  - Difference: Both keys exist only for hosts deployed by an earlier build; B still acts on the value.
  - Reason: Migration is out of scope, so B stops reading the value as A already has, and the key leaves both files in the breaking tag.
- **[docker+podman] Host directory the container mounts**: inherent
  - A: <platform>.libs.dir (none; the block is optional; Required when libs is written; safeHostPath; read-only at /app/external/libs (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1747-1760))
  - B: <platform>.container.dataDir (/opt/solace/data; Required; absolute; CheckHostPath; mounted at /var/lib/solace)
  - Difference: Different directories for different needs.
  - Reason: The connector needs vendor jars the image may not ship, and the broker needs a writable state directory, so neither tool has the other's key (B's becomes <platform>.data-dir under the spelling and shape rows).
- **[docker+podman] Memory limit on docker and podman**: new-common-key
  - A: - (no limit written; -)
  - B: <platform>.container.mem (the scaling tier's memory, for example 6898m; ^[0-9]+[bkmg]$, case-insensitive)
  - Difference: B only. On kubernetes the same thing is resources.memory in A and msgNode.mem in B.
  - Reason: <platform>.resources.memory gives the memory limit one name on all three platforms in both tools, with each sink's own unit grammar, and A gains nothing until a connector needs a limit.
- **[docker+podman] CPU pinning**: inherent
  - A: - (-; -)
  - B: <platform>.container.cpuset, <platform>.container.monitorCpuset (0 to cores-1 of the tier; 0 for the monitor; cpu list grammar; must name exactly the tier's core count; the monitor exactly one)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: The broker's scaling tier fixes a core count and wants dedicated cores, plus one for the HA monitor, and the connector has no such requirement.
- **[docker+podman] Core-dump and other process limits**: inherent
  - A: - (the unit asks for no core limit; -)
  - B: <platform>.container.ulimits.core (nofile, memlock and shmSize are fixed constants and refused as keys) (-1; -1, 0 or a positive integer that fits int64)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: These limits are the broker's own needs, already listed under Host limits in the parity body's inherent differences (M:paritydoc/body.md:202-203).
- **[docker+podman] Engine health check**: inherent
  - A: - (always on: the status script in --health mode, every 30s, timeout 10s, 3 retries, start period 60s (A:internal/dockergen/dockergen.go:287-294; A:internal/statusscript/statusscript.go:55-70); no key)
  - B: <platform>.container.healthCheck.{enabled, cmd, interval, timeout, retries, startPeriod} (off; 5s, 5s, 3, 60s; With no cmd the image tag must be 10.26 or later; durations parsed; cmd tokens checked)
  - Difference: A's check is fixed and always on; B's is opt-in with a replaceable command and settable timings.
  - Reason: The broker's readiness endpoint exists only from 10.26 and depends on the image, so B needs the switch and a replaceable command, while A's probe is the tool's own script and is always present.
- **[docker+podman+kubernetes] Read-only root filesystem (decided for A)**: inherent
  - A: - today; decided: a new key, proposed <platform>.read-only-root (decided: read-only (A:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:58); proposed: true or false)
  - B: - (always writable; kubernetes.containerSecurity.readOnlyRootFilesystem is refused by name (B:docs/configuration.md:232, 727))
  - Difference: A gains a key, B never has one.
  - Reason: The broker needs a writable root and B refuses the key by name, and gdu 6 leaves the root filesystem to each tool's own tests (M:docs/todo.md:373-374).
- **[kubernetes] Cluster context**: already-aligned
  - A: - (rides in kubernetes.command, for example kubectl --context prod; -)
  - B: - (rides in kubernetes.command; -)
  - Difference: None: neither file has a context key.
  - Reason: Decided under gdu 11: the context comes from the command tokens or the session, is resolved once and pinned, and no key is added.
- **[kubernetes] Instance name on kubernetes**: align-to-B
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.name (none; Required; DNS-1123 label; every derived name must stay within 63 characters (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:23))
  - B: kubernetes.name (none; Required; DNS-1123 label; at most 48 characters because the operator appends -pubsubplus-p-0 (B:internal/config/config.go:673))
  - Difference: A nests the name under deployment; the length limit differs with each tool's suffixes.
  - Reason: The instance name is <platform>.name in the docker and podman sections of both tools, and B has no Deployment to nest it under; the length limit is each workload's own.
- **[kubernetes] Namespace**: align-to-B
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.namespace (none; Required; DNS-1123 label (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:24))
  - B: kubernetes.namespace (none; Required; DNS-1123 label (B:internal/config/config.go:674))
  - Difference: Place only.
  - Reason: The namespace holds every object of the instance, not only the Deployment, and B's replication block already uses the same word.
- **[kubernetes] Number of pods**: inherent
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.replicas (1 (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:441-443); Above 1 is refused in standalone mode; no lower bound)
  - B: - (one pod, or three when redundancy.enabled is true; -)
  - Difference: A only.
  - Reason: A connector scales by replicas of one Deployment, while a broker is exactly one node or the fixed group of three the operator builds.
- **[kubernetes] CPU and memory on kubernetes**: new-common-key
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.resources.cpu, kubernetes.deployment.resources.memory (empty: no resources block; Kubernetes quantity; written as identical request and limit (A:internal/deploy/deploy.go:758-773))
  - B: kubernetes.msgNode.mem (kubernetes.msgNode.cpu is removed) (the scaling tier's memory, for example 3410Mi; CPU fixed by scaling.maxConnections; No load-time rule found on mem; written unquoted (B:internal/render/render.go:163-167))
  - Difference: Different block and word for the memory of the workload's pods; CPU is not settable in B.
  - Reason: kubernetes.resources.{cpu,memory} is free of both deployment and msgNode and matches the container row, with memory only in B because the broker's tier fixes its CPU.
- **[kubernetes] Node selector**: new-common-key
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.node-selector (none; A mapping; each key a Kubernetes label key, each value a label value (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:30-32))
  - B: kubernetes.placement.labelsPrimary, labelsBackup, labelsMonitor (none; Lists of "key: value" strings; each needs a colon and a key (B:internal/config/config.go:820-822))
  - Difference: Block name, key word, and a mapping against a list of strings; B has one list per broker role.
  - Reason: kubernetes.placement.node-selector as a mapping (per role in B) writes label pairs as YAML pairs in both tools, and only the split by role is forced, by the operator's per-role nodeAssignment.
- **[kubernetes] Tolerations**: new-common-key
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.tolerations (none; List of entries in Kubernetes' own shape: key, operator, value, effect, tolerationSeconds, each checked (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:34-36, 51-59))
  - B: kubernetes.placement.tolerationsPrimary, tolerationsBackup, tolerationsMonitor (none; Lists of "key[=value]:effect" strings; no load-time rule found (B:internal/config/config.go:817-819; B:internal/render/render.go:313-326))
  - Difference: Block name and entry shape; B's string cannot carry tolerationSeconds and is not checked at load.
  - Reason: kubernetes.placement.tolerations with Kubernetes' own entry shape (per role in B) reads like the Kubernetes documentation in both tools and can be checked field by field.
- **[kubernetes] Keeping pods on different nodes**: inherent
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.spread (none; none, preferred or required; refused together with affinity.podAntiAffinity (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:38-40, 61-68))
  - B: kubernetes.placement.antiAffinityNamespaces, kubernetes.placement.antiAffinityWeight ([kubernetes.namespace], weight 100: a preferred term that is always on; No rule found)
  - Difference: A offers a three-way switch for the replicas of one Deployment; B always writes one preferred term with a namespace list and a weight.
  - Reason: A spreads the replicas of one Deployment by its own app label, while B keeps the pods of three role StatefulSets, and brokers in other namespaces, apart, which needs the namespace list and the weight.
- **[kubernetes] Affinity**: align-to-A
  - A: kubernetes.deployment.affinity (unset; Kubernetes' own block as written (nodeAffinity, podAffinity, podAntiAffinity); outline and key charset checked (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:42-48))
  - B: kubernetes.placement.nodeAffinity.{preferred[].{weight,match[]}, required[]}, kubernetes.placement.podAffinity[], podAntiAffinity[] with {weight, topologyKey, matchLabels, namespaces} (none; A reduced dialect: no matchFields, no OR-ed terms, matchLabels only; applies to every role (B:internal/config/config.go:826-869))
  - Difference: A passes Kubernetes' block through; B models a subset with its own field names.
  - Reason: kubernetes.placement.affinity in Kubernetes' own shape is the full vocabulary with nothing new to learn, and B can keep its built-in term apart from it the way A already refuses spread together with podAntiAffinity.
- **[kubernetes] Service and its ports**: new-common-key
  - A: kubernetes.service.enabled, kubernetes.service.port (false; management.port on both sides; port is an integer or "servicePort:targetPort" (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:70-77; A:internal/deploy/deploy.go:775-788))
  - B: kubernetes.ports, kubernetes.loadBalancer.ip, kubernetes.loadBalancer.ipPool, kubernetes.loadBalancer.annotations (no list: the operator's default ports; type always LoadBalancer; ports entries "name=containerPort[:servicePort][/proto]" (B:internal/config/config.go:693; B:internal/render/render.go:209-225); annotations are "key: value" strings)
  - Difference: Block name. B's entry puts the container port first, while A's service.port and both tools' container ports put the outside port first. B writes annotations as strings.
  - Reason: One kubernetes.service block in both tools, with B's ports as entries of name, port, targetPort and protocol and its annotations as a mapping, ends the reversed order without silently flipping an old string entry; the port list, the LoadBalancer type and the MetalLB keys stay the broker's.
- **[kubernetes] Identity the pod runs as**: inherent
  - A: kubernetes.run-as (1000:0 (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:444-446); "uid", "uid:gid" or image; the gid is also fsGroup (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:413-416))
  - B: kubernetes.securityContext.{runAsUser, fsGroup}, kubernetes.containerSecurity.{runAsUser, runAsGroup} (omitted: the operator's defaults 1000001 and 1000002; Decimal ids up to 2147483647; 0 means the operator's default or an id the OpenShift SCC assigns (B:env/sample.yaml:284-292))
  - Difference: One uid:gid pair in A, four fields of two custom-resource blocks in B.
  - Reason: The operator renders the pod from two custom-resource blocks and gives the id 0 its own meaning, so B passes the resource's fields through, while A writes the pod's securityContext itself.
- **[kubernetes] Workload storage**: A read-only jar volume against the broker's data volumes.
  - A: kubernetes.libs.pvc.{existing
  - create.{storage, nfs.server, nfs.path}}, kubernetes.libs.download.{urls, image, pvc} (no libs volume; storage 1Gi; download image busybox:1.37 (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:451-456); Exactly one of pvc or download; exactly one of create or existing)
  - Difference: B: kubernetes.storage.{class, msgNodeSize, monNodeSize, customVolumeMount.<primary|backup|monitor>} (cluster default class; monNodeSize 5Gi; msgNodeSize mandatory unless customVolumeMount covers every node; class and customVolumeMount exclusive)
  - Reason: inherent | The connector needs a volume of vendor jars, and the broker needs per-node data volumes the operator provisions from the custom resource.
- **[kubernetes] Credentials Secret**: A says create or existing in words. In B the same name key means build-and-own or reference, depending on a key elsewhere in the file.
  - A: kubernetes.secrets.credentials.{create: true
  - existing: NAME} (absent; an error once any credential is referenced; Exactly one; a created Secret is always <name>-credentials (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:120-128))
  - Difference: B: kubernetes.adminSecret (<name>-admin when semp.adminPass is set; else none and the operator generates one; With adminPass the tool builds and later deletes the named Secret; without it the name is only referenced (B:internal/config/config.go:103-133))
  - Reason: align-to-A | With kubernetes.secrets.credentials.{create|existing} and a derived name, naming a Secret someone else made can never lead the tool to overwrite or delete it, and the absent block keeps B's operator-generated case.
- **[kubernetes] Image pull Secret**: Different place and switch. A itself uses name here but existing in its credentials and libs blocks.
  - A: kubernetes.secrets.image-pull.{name: NAME
  - create: true} (absent: no imagePullSecrets; create needs image.user and image.pass and builds <name>-image-pull; without create, name references a Secret (A:internal/validate/validate.go:1567-1579))
  - Difference: B: kubernetes.imagePullSecret (<name>-image-pull when image.user and image.pass are set; else none; Built when the credentials are present, only referenced when they are not (B:internal/config/config.go:190-221))
  - Reason: new-common-key | kubernetes.secrets.image-pull.{create: true | existing: NAME} uses the word A's other blocks already use, and the explicit switch matters most in B, where the same two image keys also drive the docker and podman login.
- **[kubernetes] TLS Secret**: align-to-A
  - A: - (the <name>-stores Secret is derived whenever the tool holds a selected store; an existing Secret is named per store in tls.stores.<name>.existing.secret.{name,key}) (derived; kubernetes.secrets.stores is retired (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:227-231))
  - B: kubernetes.tlsServerSecret (<name>-tls when tls.cert and tls.certKey are set; else no tls block; Built from the files when they are set, only referenced when they are not (B:env/sample.yaml:266-273))
  - Difference: B has a name key with the same double meaning as its other two Secret keys.
  - Reason: kubernetes.secrets.tls.{create|existing} gives B's third Secret the same switch as the other two, under the one secrets block A already has.
- **[kubernetes] Image pull policy**: align-to-B
  - A: - (no imagePullPolicy line: Kubernetes' default; -)
  - B: kubernetes.imagePullPolicy (empty, written as IfNotPresent (B:internal/render/render.go:89-95); empty, Always, IfNotPresent or Never)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: B's key, spelled image-pull-policy, is the common name and A takes it only if an operator ever needs Always or Never, since nothing in the connector forces the difference.
- **[kubernetes] Update strategy**: inherent
  - A: - (the Deployment's rolling update; -)
  - B: kubernetes.updateStrategy (automatedRolling; automatedRolling or manualPodRestart)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: It is a field of the operator's custom resource that tells the operator how to roll broker pods, and A's Deployment has no counterpart.
- **[kubernetes] Service account**: inherent
  - A: - (automountServiceAccountToken: false (A:internal/deploy/deploy.go:562-565); -)
  - B: kubernetes.serviceAccount (none: the block is omitted; No rule found; written unquoted (B:internal/render/render.go:100-103))
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: The connector never calls the Kubernetes API and mounts no token, while the operator runs broker pods under an account the custom resource can name.
- **[kubernetes] Pod labels and annotations**: align-to-B
  - A: - (only the tool's own le-mode and role labels on the pod template; -)
  - B: kubernetes.podAnnotations, kubernetes.podLabels (none; Mappings; no rule found; rendered quoted with sorted keys (B:internal/config/config.go:688-689))
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: B's keys, spelled pod-annotations and pod-labels, are the common names and A takes them only if a connector ever needs them; the tools' own ownership labels never go into them (M:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:200).
- **[kubernetes] Operator installation**: inherent
  - A: - (-; -)
  - B: kubernetes.operator.{image, namespace, watchNamespaces, watchBrokerNs, cpu, mem} (solace/pubsubplus-eventbroker-operator:1.4.2, pubsubplus-operator-system, empty, true, 500m, 512Mi; image grammar; DNS-1123 labels; Kubernetes quantities)
  - Difference: B only.
  - Reason: Only solace-util deploys through an operator; if the resources row is accepted its cpu and mem become operator.resources.{cpu,memory}.
- **[kubernetes] Second image and the registry prefix**: inherent
  - A: kubernetes.libs.download.image (busybox:1.37 (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:454-456); A full reference; SafeToken; image.repo is not prefixed)
  - B: kubernetes.operator.image (solace/pubsubplus-eventbroker-operator:1.4.2; Repository path with optional tag and digest; image.registry is prefixed at render)
  - Difference: A's second image is a full reference; B's takes the main image's registry as a prefix.
  - Reason: The operator image comes from the same vendor registry as the broker image and is mirrored with it, while the connector's download helper is an unrelated third-party image that often lives elsewhere.

### 4.2 Owner questions

1. Which spelling do both env files use for a multi-word key: kebab-case (A) or camelCase (B)? Recommended: kebab-case for every key a tool defines, with -env as the reference suffix; Kubernetes' or the custom resource's own field names stay as they are only inside a block passed on in the API's shape (a toleration entry, an affinity block, a Service port entry, B's securityContext fields). Cost: B renames 71 yaml tags (its convert command and the generator page follow); A changes none in the platform sections. The other way round A renames 30 tags, including every connection key that mirrors a Spring property.
2. What is the network key called and where does it sit? Recommended: a scalar docker.network and podman.network directly in the section, beside ports. Docker takes bridge, host, none; podman takes private, bridge, pasta, slirp4netns, host, none; the lists live in M and each tool passes its key name. No default is filled into the config: an absent key writes the platform's non-host default (the project network with driver: bridge; Network=private). Kubernetes has no key. B's network.mode and network.ports become network and ports, and B's filled default bridge goes, because the word bridge now writes network_mode: bridge or Network=bridge. The alternative is to keep B's block (network.mode and network.ports) and move A's ports under it.
3. Is a ports list together with network host or none an error? Recommended: yes, a load error in both tools. B checks the list today and silently writes nothing in host mode; podman itself discards the mappings with a warning.
4. Does B's container block flatten into the section, and does A's kubernetes.deployment block dissolve, so the instance is <platform>.name on all three platforms in both tools and the namespace is kubernetes.namespace? Recommended: yes. B: docker.name, docker.run-as, docker.data-dir and so on. A: kubernetes.name, kubernetes.namespace, kubernetes.replicas, kubernetes.resources, and node-selector, tolerations, spread and affinity under kubernetes.placement. The minimum is the name and namespace move.
5. Does the container name follow one grammar on docker and podman? Recommended: a DNS-1123 label in both tools. It narrows B, which accepts capitals, dots, underscores and 100 characters today; the defaults connector and solace stay.
6. What are the image keys? Recommended: image.registry (host and optional port only; empty means Docker Hub and its auths key), image.repository, image.tag. The key repo goes from both files because it means the registry in A and the repository in B. A digest in image.tag stays A only. The alternative for the middle key is A's image.name.
7. Does A log in to the registry on docker and podman when image.user and image.pass are set, as B does? Recommended: yes, through one new M executor (containercli.Client.Login, password on stdin, after the redeploy question), and B gains image.user-env. This removes the Registry entry from the parity body's inherent differences and adds a method to the catalogue of design 3.4. If the answer is no, the row stays inherent and A's keys keep serving the kubernetes pull Secret only.
8. Does B take A's run-as key and grammar on docker and podman (uid, uid:gid or the word image; a bare uid means gid 0), keeping its own defaults? Recommended: yes. On kubernetes B keeps the custom resource's four id fields, recorded as inherent.
9. Does B gain docker.restart and podman.restart? Recommended: yes, with A's word lists (moved into M beside the network lists) and always as B's default on both; A's docker default unless-stopped stays, and the default difference is written down, not aligned.
10. Does A gain docker.compose, and is the docker allowlist the same in both tools? Recommended: yes to both: A gains the key with the default docker.command plus compose, both tools accept a list as well as a string for every command key, and both allow docker, docker-compose and nerdctl without --allow-command. If nerdctl is not wanted in A, drop it from B instead of keeping two lists.
11. Does B drop podman.quadletDir, and do both tools drop the legacy base directory key? Recommended: yes to both. The unit directory is what podman.QuadletDir returns in both tools; the parity body's Quadlet directory row loses its override clause. B's baseDir clean-up is a fallback for old deployments, which migration being out of scope rules out.
12. Do both tools name Kubernetes Secrets the same way? Recommended: one block kubernetes.secrets with credentials, image-pull and (B only) tls, each taking create: true or existing: NAME; a created Secret always has the derived name; an existing one is never written or deleted. A renames image-pull.name to existing. B drops adminSecret, tlsServerSecret and imagePullSecret, and with them the custom name for a Secret the tool builds. Note that A derives its stores Secret with no switch at all; if the owner prefers that implicit rule for content the tool always holds, B's tls entry needs only existing.
13. Is the memory and CPU setting <platform>.resources.{cpu,memory} everywhere? Recommended: yes. A: kubernetes.resources. B: kubernetes.resources.memory, docker.resources.memory, podman.resources.memory and kubernetes.operator.resources; B's CPU stays fixed by the scaling tier.
14. Do both tools write placement in Kubernetes' own shapes under kubernetes.placement? Recommended: yes: node-selector as a mapping and tolerations as Kubernetes' entries (per role in B, for example placement.primary.tolerations), and affinity as Kubernetes' own block in both. B's string forms and its reduced affinity dialect go; its built-in anti-affinity keys stay and, as in A, are refused together with a podAntiAffinity of the operator's own.
15. Does B's Service configuration move under kubernetes.service, with the outside port first? Recommended: yes: kubernetes.service.ports as entries of name, port, targetPort and protocol, kubernetes.service.annotations as a mapping, kubernetes.service.load-balancer.{ip, ip-pool}. A keeps service.enabled and its single service.port. An old B string entry then fails to decode, which is safer than swapping its two numbers.
16. Which port grammar does M hold for docker and podman? Recommended: B's: [ip:]host:container[/tcp|/udp] with ranges, always naming both sides. A's bare integer goes, because the engines read a bare port as a container port on a random host port.
17. Do the tools keep a detector for every removed or renamed key? Recommended: no. Both decode strictly (A's CR-0046), the breaking tag removes the existing detectors and adds none for the gdu 19 renames, and each configuration.md keeps one table of renamed keys. No rename proposed here reuses an old key with a new meaning or a compatible shape, so every old file fails loudly without a detector.
18. What is A's read-only root key called? Recommended: <platform>.read-only-root, true or false, default true, in A's docker, podman and kubernetes sections. B has no such key and keeps refusing a read-only root.
19. Are the rows marked inherent accepted as the list for the parity body's Inherent differences, and the one-sided keys left one-sided? Recommended: yes. Inherent: image digest, TLS material, application credentials, HA model, scaling, replication access, libs against data directories, cpuset, process limits, the health check switch, read-only root, replicas, spread against the built-in anti-affinity, pod identity on kubernetes, storage, update strategy, service account, operator keys and the second image's registry rule. One-sided and not forced: B's image-pull-policy, pod-annotations and pod-labels, which A takes only on need.

### 4.3 Notes

How to read the rows - A = A, B = B, M = M. A file:line in a row is a line I opened in this run. A fact with no cite comes from the two reviewers' listings and was not re-opened. - For a key only one tool has, align-to-A or align-to-B means: that tool's key, in the agreed spelling, is the common name, and the other tool takes it only if it ever offers the setting. The reason says so each time. - "inherent" rows carry the workload reason and are the candidates for the parity body's Inherent differences. - Read-only: nothing was run, edited or created. Resulting shape if every recommendation is taken - Shared: image.{registry, repository, tag, user, user-env, pass, pass-env}, timezone. - docker (both): command, compose, project-name, name, run-as, restart, network, ports. A adds read-only-root, libs.dir. B adds data-dir, resources.memory, cpuset, monitor-cpuset, ulimits.core, health-check.{enabled, cmd, interval, timeout, retries, start-period}. - podman (both): command, rootful, name, run-as, restart, network, ports, plus the same tool-specific keys as docker. - kubernetes (both): command, name, namespace, resources, placement, service, secrets.{credentials, image-pull}.{create|existing}. A adds run-as, read-only-root, replicas, placement.spread, service.{enabled, port}, libs. B adds securityContext and containerSecurity ids, placement.<role>, the anti-affinity keys, service.{ports, annotations, load-balancer}, secrets.tls, storage, operator, image-pull-policy, update-strategy, service-account, pod-annotations, pod-labels. Findings that bear on the decisions 1. The decided project key is not built in either tool. A writes it as the file's name: and documents the COMPOSE_PROJECT_NAME override (A:docs/configuration.md:857-858). B derives it from the container name and keeps the override on purpose (B:internal/render/render.go:1056-1063). M's ComposeUp and ComposeDown take no project, and their doc says the project is the file's own name: key (M:containercli/containercli.go:97-113). 2. Neither tool passes --remove-orphans (M:containercli/containercli.go:102-113), so two instances can share one project as decided. A compose down for one instance will still try to remove the shared project network; not checked on a host. 3. B fills network.mode with bridge for both sections even when the key is absent (B:internal/config/load.go:260-261). Under the 2026-11-15 words that default must go, and an env file that says bridge changes meaning: network_mode: bridge leaves the project network, and Network=bridge is not rootless podman's default. 4. B writes the pull Secret's auths key from image.registry as it stands (B:internal/k8s/secrets.go:254-256). With credentials set and no registry the key is the empty string; A writes https://index.docker.io/v1/ (A:internal/spec/image.go:9, 83-88). M's Secret renderer needs one rule; A's is the working one. 5. B resolves every Env reference in Load (B:internal/config/secretref.go:56-80), so remove fails on an unset variable. gdu 3 decided that remove resolves no credential. 6. A's own review already decided strict decode (CR-0046), new network and root filesystem keys (CR-0042) and an optional sha256 on each libs.download entry (CR-0041) (A:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:56-62). CR-0042 still says "defaulting to bridge"; the 2026-11-14 and 2026-11-15 decisions in M replaced that default. 7. Parity body rows that change: "Quadlet directory" names podman.quadletDir as an override (M:paritydoc/body.md:148), and "Registry" is listed as inherent (M:paritydoc/body.md:200-201). gdu 19 also adds a row for the shared keys and gdu 6 a row for the network mode. 8. B values with no load-time grammar that are written unquoted into the custom resource, per B's reviewer: kubernetes.serviceAccount, storage.class, msgNode.mem, customVolumeMount claim names, loadBalancer.ip and ipPool, placement.antiAffinityNamespaces. They are not key-name issues, but the Custom resource body stays B's own text after rendering moves, so gdu 4's "held to the sink's grammar" has to be met in B. 9. A's unchecked values, per A's reviewer: management.port has no range check and deployment.replicas no lower bound. 10. Port order: A's kubernetes.service.port is servicePort:targetPort (A:internal/spec/kubernetes.go:70-77), B's kubernetes.ports entry is containerPort[:servicePort] (B:internal/config/config.go:693). Both tools' container ports are host:container. The Service row removes the reversed case. 11. A's restart comment says systemd's always already means "unless you stopped it" (A:internal/spec/targets.go:29-33). That holds within one boot; a unit with an [Install] section starts again at boot. This is why the restart question keeps each tool's default instead of aligning them. What M gains from this table (for the plan of the breaking tag, not decided here) - Word lists beside the compose and quadlet packages: the network words (decided) and, if accepted, the restart words. - One port type, one container-name grammar (the DNS-1123 label it already has), one registry payload for the pull Secret. - containercli.Client.Login, if the registry question is answered yes. - The key name is always passed in by the tool, as rootless.Decide does today. Not verified - Line numbers in the reviewers' listings that I did not open, among them B's validate.go beyond 380-420 and 1696-1712, hostpath.go, scaling.go, and A's validate.go outside 40-75, 395-436, 1518-1582, 1622-1760 and 1805-1862. - Whether the operator's nodeAssignment accepts every field of a standard affinity block; I read only the start of B's writer (B:internal/render/render.go:289-345). - Engine behaviour stated from knowledge, not run: compose down on a shared project network, podman discarding PublishPort under Network=host, a bare port in docker's publish syntax, the quadlet generator's search directories, and Network=private on the oldest supported podman (already an owner check under gdu 6).

### 4.4 Verifier

- **Alignment row 'Key spelling': ruleA, the B tag count, and the rule's boundary**
  - Was: ruleA: 'A camelCase spelling of a tool key is an error (A:docs/troubleshooting.md:269-270)'. keyB: '71 camelCase yaml tags in B:internal/config'. Owner question 1 keeps camelCase only inside API-shaped blocks, naming B's securityContext fields.
  - Should be: In A that error exists only inside a solace:/mq: block (connections, workflow sides, leader-election session, mq-defaults). Under docker, podman and kubernetes a camelCase or misspelled key is dropped silently today, as the alignment's own third row says. B has 75 distinct camelCase yaml tags in non-test internal/config (69 live, 6 kept only to be refused: cliScriptsFolder, composeFile, diagDir, maxPool, shmSize, readOnlyRootFilesystem), plus the hand-decoded key fileExt and 8 snake_case scaling aliases that the row gives no rule for. The boundary is also inconsistent: update-strategy, service-account, pod-labels, pod-annotations and image-pull-policy are one-to-one CR fields and go kebab, while the securityContext and containerSecurity ids stay camelCase although containerSecurity is B's own word (the CR's is brokerContainerSecurity).
  - Evidence: A:docs/troubleshooting.md:267-270 (the sentence is about passthrough keys); A:internal/validate/extra.go:13-21,43-74; A:internal/spec/env.go:52-56; grep of yaml tags over B:internal/config non-test files (75 with a lower-then-upper pair); B:internal/config/config.go:687-690,944; B:internal/config/scaling.go:231-238; B:internal/render/render.go:266-276
- **Alignment row 'Secret given as a literal or as an environment reference': B's list**
  - Was: B pairs listed: image.pass, semp.adminPass, redundancy.psk, tls.certPassphrase, additionalUsers[].password
  - Should be: B's one list of pairs also holds semp.monitorPass / monitorPassEnv and replication.sites[].via.semp.pass / passEnv; all are resolved in Load. The replication pair has a third form, passSecret, so its rule is 'exactly one of three', not the two-key pair rule. All of these are renamed by the -env suffix.
  - Evidence: B:internal/config/secretref.go:23-27,39-46,68-80; B:internal/config/config.go:581-583; B:internal/config/validate.go:1206-1224
- **Alignment rows 'Registry host' and 'Image repository path': the value rule is left open**
  - Was: image.registry (A's host-only rule, Docker Hub key when empty) plus new image.repository; 'B lets the registry ride inside the repository' recorded as a difference only
  - Should be: The recommendation must also say that image.repository carries no registry host. Today both tools accept one there: B by grammar, A because image.name is only SafeToken. With the host in the repository and registry empty, the pull Secret's auths key is wrong in both (A writes the Docker Hub key, B writes an empty key), so M's one registry payload is wrong unless the rule is stated. Cost to B of host-only registry: its documented path prefix (ghcr.io/solace) goes, and kubernetes.operator.image must then carry that path itself.
  - Evidence: A:internal/validate/validate.go:1547-1555; A:internal/spec/image.go:83-88; B:internal/config/artifactvalues.go:32-38; B:internal/k8s/secrets.go:253-262; B:internal/k8s/operator.go:176-181; B:docs/configuration.md:182,201
- **Alignment row 'What the registry credential does on docker and podman' (align-to-B) and owner question 7**
  - Was: Reason: 'A pulls the image to read its entrypoint and so needs the host logged in anyway'
  - Should be: A pulls only on docker, only when the deploy carries a created store and the engine lacks the image; it never pulls on podman. So the reason holds for one case. Two costs are not stated: a login stores the registry password in the engine's own auth file, against A's documented rule that no credential value is in any file the tool leaves on disk (engine behaviour stated from knowledge, not run); and existing A files that set image.user/image.pass for the kubernetes pull Secret would start logging in on docker and podman with no key change. Still an owner choice; the row should carry these.
  - Evidence: A:cmd/solmq-conn-util/main.go:825-846; A:internal/runner/runner.go:347-357 (only caller found by grep is main.go:837); A:docs/configuration.md:974-979,1039-1042; B:internal/container/manager.go:451-477; M:paritydoc/body.md:200-201
- **Owner question 17: 'No rename proposed here reuses an old key with a new meaning ... every old file fails loudly'**
  - Was: Stated without condition
  - Should be: True for the renames only if A decodes strictly first (CR-0046 is decided, not built): until then image.repo, kubernetes.deployment.replicas/resources/node-selector/tolerations/spread/affinity vanish silently in A, and a dropped image.repo pulls from Docker Hub. One proposal changes an unrenamed key's effect silently: image.user/image.pass in A gain a login on docker and podman. A B file with a bare uid moves from 'User= only' to 'gid 0' once rewritten as run-as.
  - Evidence: A:internal/spec/env.go:52-56; A:docs/review/2026-11-13-decisions.md:62; A:internal/spec/image.go:59-76; B:internal/render/render.go:892,904-907; A:internal/spec/runas.go:47-63
- **Alignment row 'Engine command' (align-to-B) and owner question 10: one docker allowlist {docker, docker-compose, nerdctl} in both tools**
  - Was: 'one allowlist per platform should hold in both tools once both run the same M commands'
  - Should be: B's single list is already too wide for docker.command: 'docker.command: docker-compose' passes the guard, and the derived compose command is then 'docker-compose compose' while every engine call (login, inspect) runs docker-compose. The lists should be per field: command {docker, nerdctl}, compose {docker, nerdctl, docker-compose}. For A the wider list is unverified: its compose file needs Compose v2.23.1 features (inline configs, environment secrets) and it runs 'image inspect --format' and 'image pull'; whether nerdctl and a standalone compose binary provide these was not checked on a host.
  - Evidence: B:internal/config/execguard.go:38-42,49,71,76-83,311-318; A:docs/configuration.md:836-842; A:internal/runner/runner.go:330-357
- **Alignment row 'Identity the container runs as on docker and podman' (align-to-A) and owner question 8**
  - Was: B takes A's run-as grammar: uid, uid:gid or the word image
  - Should be: B cannot take the word image as it stands: B chowns the data directory to the run user before deploy (podman unshare chown rootless, chown otherwise), so it needs numeric ids. B must refuse image or resolve it to the image's 1000001. The bare-uid meaning also changes for B (today User= with no Group=, and chown of the owner only; under A's rule gid 0).
  - Evidence: B:internal/container/manager.go:417-443; B:internal/render/render.go:892,904-907; B:internal/config/load.go:289-324; A:internal/spec/runas.go:47-63; A:internal/podmangen/podmangen.go:160-163
- **Alignment row 'Quadlet unit directory' (align-to-A): the reason, and B's quadletDir rule**
  - Was: 'the override mostly lets a file name a directory the unit is never loaded from'; B rule 'CheckHostPath; never rebased'
  - Should be: Dropping the key is defensible, but the reason is overstated: M's own doc says QuadletDir is the directory written, not every directory podman reads, and that the user manager does not see an XDG_CONFIG_HOME set only in a shell profile; B documents the key as the way to bypass a failed lookup. Without the key the operator must fix the environment instead. Separately, B's docs say a relative quadletDir is refused, but the code has no absoluteness check for it (only baseDir and dataDir), so a relative value is written relative to the working directory today.
  - Evidence: M:podman/podman.go:127-139; B:docs/configuration.md:86,112-115,215; B:internal/config/validate.go:313-326,1536,1575; B:internal/config/hostpath.go:227-230; B:internal/container/manager.go:792
- **Alignment rows 'Memory limit on docker and podman' and 'CPU and memory on kubernetes' (new common key <platform>.resources.memory)**
  - Was: 'one name on all three platforms in both tools, with each sink's own unit grammar'
